Let’s summarize this chapter!
Summary
This chapter examined the essential building blocks of data architectures in Azure, mapping core capabilities—such as ingestion, processing, storage, and serving—to corresponding Azure services. We explored how these capabilities support established patterns such as Lambda, Kappa, and Medallion, and how services such as Azure Databricks, Synapse Analytics, Event Hubs, Stream Analytics, and Data Explorer play distinct roles depending on the scenario.
We also covered HTAP, identifying Synapse Link for Cosmos DB as the only Azure-native service that currently enables real-time analytics directly on transactional data without data duplication between the OLTP and OLAP systems.
Beyond platform services, we reviewed infrastructure enablers such as the SHIR, MVN, and VNDG to bridge secure connectivity gaps between cloud and on-premises or private environments.
The use of Cosmos DB as a flagship OLTP store was also explored in detail through an example of a multi-tenant mobile app for managing wine cellars, emphasizing best practices such as partitioning, indexing strategy, and leveraging change feed for aggregate updates.
The chapter concluded with a retail use case involving smart fridges. A Kappa architecture was implemented using Terraform and .NET, with telemetry ingested through Event Hubs, processed in real time with Stream Analytics, and stored in Azure Data Explorer. Anomalies were forwarded to an Azure function for alerting, illustrating a complete, scalable IoT analytics pipeline.
In the next chapter, we’ll explore Azure’s vast AI ecosystem with a focus on generative AI.

8
Artificial Intelligence Architecture
This chapter is arguably the most challenging, given the inherently complex and fast-evolving nature of AI, making it difficult to provide insights that will remain relevant over time. To address this, we will concentrate on foundational concepts that are less likely to undergo significant changes in the months and years ahead. This should help guide your future design decisions.
We will begin by distinguishing between generative and non-generative AI (traditional), highlighting scenarios where
traditional AI remains the appropriate choice. We will then shift our focus to
Generative AI
(
GenAI
), exploring both well-established patterns and emerging but future-proof ones.
More specifically, we’ll look at the following topics:
The AI Architecture Map
Zooming in on traditional AI
Zooming in on generative AI
Our use case will focus on the design aspects, as any provided code would likely become outdated before the book is even published. Instead, the next chapter will provide a list of useful reference implementations to help you progress in your AI journey.
Technical requirements
There are no technical requirements for this chapter. Maps and diagrams are available at
https://github.com/PacktPublishing/The-Azure-Cloud-Native-Architecture-Mapbook-Second-Edition/tree/main/Chapter08
.
The Azure AI Architecture Map
The Azure AI
Architecture Map, shown in
Figure 8.1
, should help you deal with the typical duties of an AI architect:
Figure 8.1 – The Azure AI Architecture Map

Figure 8.1
), you can download the PDF file available at

Figure 8.1
has the following top-level groups:
Traditional AI
: This section
groups all traditional AI services that are based on pre-trained models and that allow custom training to meet business-specific requirements. This type of AI service is typically used for classification, prediction, and responding to inputs in a predictable way, rather than generating creative outputs.
Generative AI
: This section
groups all AI services that are capable of creating new content that resembles human-like content. The AI waves that have shaken the world in recent years are all closely tied to GenAI. We’ll dedicate most of this chapter to GenAI.
Patterns
: This section
brings together several AI patterns and explains how to best leverage Azure to implement them.
Let’s get started with the traditional AI services
Zooming in on traditional AI services
You might ask why traditional AI services are still relevant in the age of GenAI. There are several good reasons. GenAI’s
creative outputs aren’t always appropriate, and the computational
demands of hosting
Large Language Models
(
LLMs
) can be
significant, although
Small Language Models
(
SLMs
) are less demanding. In contrast, traditional AI offers more predictable results and is often a far more cost-effective solution.
When dealing with business cases that demand high precision, have a well-defined scope, and require minimal custom training, traditional AI remains a strong candidate.
Most Azure AI services support custom training with your own data—whether it’s text, images, or audio—enabling the resulting models to perform effectively in real-world situations.
Figure 8.2
illustrates the traditional AI services that are part of Azure.
Figure 8.2 –Traditional AI services
The diagram in
Figure 8.2
has a few subsections, among which is
Machine Learning
.
Machine learning
Azure Machine Learning
(
Azure ML
), a native
offering, is by far the AI
service that requires the most true AI expertise. It supports both
supervised and unsupervised learning, enabling a wide range of capabilities. While GenAI outperforms it in many areas, Azure ML remains a strong choice for prediction-driven scenarios and unsupervised learning.
Supervised learning requires human-based labeled data. In a nutshell, labeling is the process of assigning labels
to raw data to help the model make predictions when inferencing. For example, if you had to build a solution that helps your employees report IT incidents and automatically detects the incident category based on the description, you could proceed with the following steps:
Collect historical data
: You would extract historical data from your
Information Technology Service Management
(
ITSM
) database.
Prepare data
: Begin by cleaning and organizing the data, as data quality is often suboptimal in most systems. Enhancing data quality is essential before training any model—after all, as the saying goes,
garbage in, garbage out
.
Label prepared data
: You would then assign a category to each incident description. This labeled dataset would serve as the ground truth for training your model.
Train the model
: You would choose the appropriate algorithm for training data. This could be one of the multiclass classification options.
Evaluate the model
: The last step before launching the model and making it available to applications would be to evaluate its accuracy and precision, using well-known techniques such as
precision
,
recall
, and
F-score
.
Azure ML has
tools that help accomplish and complete each of the preceding steps.
On the other hand, unsupervised learning doesn’t require data labeling as it involves looking at patterns and
deviations in behavior across a large dataset. If we look at the previous example, we could extract our ITSM data and use unsupervised learning to identify patterns and outliers in data clusters of IT incidents. This step could help clean our data before training our supervised model.
Today, with the rise of GenAI, Microsoft’s focus has shifted a bit, and many practitioners complain about outdated examples and SDKs for Azure ML. A solid alternative is Azure Databricks, especially if you are already using Spark.
Natural language processing
You may not realize it but
Natural Language Processing
(
NLP
) is a very complex field of AI. Natural language is
inherently ambiguous—it includes humor, irony, homonyms, polysemy, and more. Even among humans, miscommunication is common. We often need to
repeat ourselves or clarify our intent by providing additional context.
The most challenging
aspect of NLP is capturing semantics—that is, understanding the meaning of words within their specific context. In traditional AI, we rely on techniques such as
tokenization
. Tokens can represent entire words, subwords, or even individual characters such as punctuation marks. AI models are trained on tokenized text and automatically break down our inputs into tokens during processing. Tokenization is a fundamental technique used by every NLP practitioner. However, tokens alone are not enough to extract semantics. A very popular NLP technique
used to extract semantics is
Part-of-Speech
(
POS
)
tagging
. This technique involves identifying words and their grammatical roles within a sentence, and it remains relevant today. While tokens represent individual, context-free segments of text, tags provide a more comprehensive understanding by revealing the grammatical structure of the sentence.
For example, take the following sentence:
I am Stephane Eyskens, an Azure Cloud Architect.
The corresponding tags would be as follows:
"I" - Pronoun (PRP)
"am" - Verb (VBP)
"Stephane" - Noun (NNP)
"Eyskens" - Noun (NNP)
"," - Punctuation (",")
"an" - Determiner (DT)
"Azure" - Adjective (JJ)
"Cloud" - Noun (NN)
"Architect" - Noun (NN)
. (period) - Punctuation (PUNCT)
Once we have identified each word and its grammatical role in a sentence, we gain a deeper understanding
of the text. Punctuation also plays
a crucial role—symbols such as
.
,
!
, and
?
help indicate sentence boundaries, questions, and emphasis. POS tagging is often performed using stochastic
methods such as
Hidden Markov Models
(
HMMs
), which excel at predicting the most likely sequence of letters, words, tags, or phonemes based on transition probabilities.
However, we still need to refine the phrase further. For example, the word
am
in the preceding sentence is derived from the verb
to be
, like many others, such as
been
,
be
,
is
, and
was
. To identify the parent entity, we rely
on the
lemmatization
technique, which helps reduce noise by identifying the
lemma
of each word. Lemmatization applies not only to verbs but also to nouns, which can have various forms depending on number, gender, and other factors. For instance, the lemma of
mice
is
mouse
. In the preceding example, only the verb
am
differs from its base form. When combined with POS tagging, lemmatization already provides a solid foundation for understanding the core meaning of a text.
Yet, the work doesn’t stop there. As mentioned earlier, language is inherently complex. A given text may include proper nouns, such as names of people, cities, or countries, as well as concepts such as
project management
—all of which are considered
entities
. Identifying these requires the use of
Named Entity Recognition
(
NER
), a technique specifically designed to detect and classify such elements in text.
NER identifies
words or phrases in text that refer to specific, real-world concepts. For example,
Stephane Eyskens
is a person,
Azure
is a cloud platform, and
Cloud Architect
is a job title—each mapped to broader categories such as
Person
,
Cloud Platform
, and
Job
. NER remains a key component of modern NLP pipelines, as recognized entities can be extracted from user input and passed as parameters to downstream systems. For instance, with Azure’s Conversational Language Understanding service, entities are automatically captured from a user query and forwarded to the application code handling the request. This allows developers to define and train models
to extract custom entities, such as a ServiceNow ticket incident number. Even in a GenAI context, entities are captured and passed as
parameters to functions during a function-calling process.
All these NLP techniques are applied regardless of the end goal—whether it’s building conversational agents, performing sentiment analysis, or extracting keywords and key phrases from text. Here is a mapping between NLP activities and use cases:
NLP Activities
Use Cases
Azure Services
Keywords, key phrases extraction
Document tagging, metadata extraction
AI Language
Sentiment analysis
Product evaluation, marketing, reviews
AI Language
NER + POS tagging + lemmatization
Intent detection, conversational
AI Language + Conversational Language Understanding
All NLP activities
Translation
AI Translator
Table 8.1 – NLP-related use cases and corresponding Azure services
While traditional AI services remain a solid choice for their fast inference, efficient compute usage, and proven capabilities, their GenAI counterparts often surpass them within the NLP domain—with little to no training required. However, if you are in a business with a very specific jargon and specific entities, you can
train the
Conversational Language Understanding
service and achieve high precision and high speed at low costs.
Let’s explore another dimension, namely the realm of computer vision.
Optical character recognition, vision, and document extraction
Optical Character Recognition
(
OCR
) is the
process of extracting
text from images. For example, your organization may handle a large volume of documents—such as PowerPoint files, PDFs, or Visio diagrams—that include images containing embedded text (knowledge). You might also receive scanned documents from customers or partners, such as invoices or purchase orders, where text extraction is needed to enable automated processing. OCR is a computer vision task that consists of interpreting each pixel in a given image to recognize text. Among the traditional AI services, Azure ships with AI Vision and AI Custom Vision, which both are able to perform OCR tasks but also image recognition to help categorize and classify images.
For example, you might self-host an instance of an AI Custom Vision model along with an IoT Edge module used by cameras (IoT Edge devices) on industrial assembly lines to detect potential product defects before packaging. In this scenario, you would train the Custom Vision model with hundreds or even thousands of images, including both flawless and defective products. Once deployed, the model performs real-time inferences on incoming images, classifying them based on the training it received to flag anomalies as they occur on the production line. For general-purpose vision requirements, you may rely on AI Vision alone.
AI Vision can be useful for extracting text from small, unstructured documents. However, for scenarios involving semi-structured or larger documents, such as forms, invoices, or contracts, you should consider using AI Document Intelligence, which is better suited for handling complex layouts and extracting structured information at scale.
AI Document Intelligence comes with predefined models that represent most document types. Additionally, you can create custom extraction and classification models using Document Intelligence Studio, to adhere to enterprise- and industry-specific documents. Classification is interesting in any document-routing scenario. For example, customers might send documents through a mobile app you’ve developed. The first step upon receiving a document would typically be to identify its type and perform an initial validation, since you have no control over what end users might submit. Depending on the result, the document could be rejected or routed to the appropriate processing service—especially if you support multiple document types.
Once the document type has been identified, you can extract its contents using a built-in or custom extraction model for further processing.
Document Intelligence
is also very often used in
Retrieval Augmented Generation
(
RAG
)-based applications to feed the knowledge databases used by AI agents to leverage organizational data.
Table 8.2
summarizes a few vision use cases and their corresponding Azure AI services:
Vision
Use Cases
Azure Services
Image classification and recognition
Generic purpose or industry-specific images
AI Vision, AI Custom Vision
Document
classification, validation, and extraction
Knowledge mining, document processing, document storage, and so on
Document Intelligence
OCR
Automated invoice processing, employee receipts, KYC document validation, insurance claims, and so on
AI Custom Vision, Document Intelligence
Table 8.2 – Vision-related use cases and corresponding Azure services
Let’s
now look at other AI capabilities, still in
the realm of traditional AI.
Other AI capabilities
Azure AI Search
is a general-purpose search service increasingly
used as a vector database and a hybrid search engine, combining the strengths of both vector- and keyword-based search. While Cosmos DB and Azure SQL also offer capabilities for full-text and vector search, Azure AI Search remains the most versatile for search-centric scenarios. We will explain later in this chapter the concept of a vector database and hybrid search.
Lastly, Azure AI Speech
can be used for anything related to
Text-to-Speech
(
TTS
) and
Speech-to-Text
(
STT
).
Let’s now
explore the world of GenAI.
Zooming in on generative AI
Figure 8.3
illustrates the
GenAI options available in Azure:
Figure 8.3 – Generative AI
We’re going to
start with the enablers that will allow you to get started in your Azure AI journey.
GenAI enablers
Azure AI Foundry is a one-stop shop for all AI-related needs. It provides a unified portal where users can explore
a catalog of over 11,000 models, including a Microsoft-curated subset. These curated models are production-ready and have undergone extensive internal compliance and quality checks to meet enterprise standards. AI Foundry also allows you to select and test a model, as well as evaluate its performance over time. The GitHub Marketplace is a free alternative that offers similar features, though with usage limits enforced through throttling mechanisms.
AI Foundry isn’t limited to the portal, as it also includes a full SDK to support the development of intelligent applications. Microsoft’s complementary offering, the Semantic Kernel framework, is available for free and provides developers with full flexibility in how they build AI-driven solutions. Microsoft offers guidance at
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-365/agents-sdk/choose-agent-solution
to help choose the most suitable solution based on the specific use case.
Semantic Kernel is an enterprise-grade, versatile framework that supports AI orchestration and offers a wide range of connectors to vector databases. Organizations with a strong .NET footprint often choose Semantic Kernel, while Python-based teams typically prefer LangChain due to its native ecosystem integration. Python is the de facto programming language for data and AI, which is why many data and AI engineers are exclusively proficient in Python.
In practice, developers from the .NET or Java ecosystem are generally accustomed to principles such as SOLID, DRY, clean architecture, and clean code, which are deeply embedded in their development culture. They are also
very well acquainted with unit and integration testing. This is partly due to the fact that .NET/Java were designed from the ground up as object-oriented platforms. In contrast, Python, originally conceived as a scripting language, has historically encouraged a more flexible and less rigid development style.
What I observed in the field is that, when building broader solutions that incorporate AI capabilities, there can be a noticeable quality gap between deliverables produced by Python developers and those by .NET/Java developers. This often stems from differing development practices, architectural disciplines, and tooling standards inherent to each ecosystem.
That said, these are general trends rather than hard rules—you can find highly structured Python developers as well as poorly disciplined .NET developers. Nevertheless, I felt it was important to highlight this cultural difference, as it can lead to frictions and tensions between teams that should not be underestimated. It’s also worth noting that Microsoft provides a Python SDK for Semantic Kernel, offering teams the flexibility to adopt the framework regardless of their preferred development language.
Important note
In August 2025, questions were raised about the future of Semantic Kernel, which would transition from general availability to maintenance mode. Microsoft will focus on a new framework. It’s worth following this GitHub issue:
https://github.com/microsoft/semantic-kernel/discussions/12779
.
Model capabilities
For any NLP scenario, Azure OpenAI’s GPT family is typically the best LLM choice. For cases that have a well-defined
scope, an SLM might be a preferred option. The Phi family is positioned as a cost-friendly and performance-driven alternative. GPT-4o is the most advanced multimodal model available through Azure OpenAI. It is also very proficient for code generation. Additionally, it is the only model that features a real-time API for speech and audio.
Azure OpenAI also features speech-specific models across the TTS and Whisper families, as well as vision-specific families such as DALL-E. Of course, many other models are made available through AI Foundry, but we are focusing mostly on what’s available through Azure OpenAI.
The next section
explores the infrastructure enablers that we can take advantage of to host and build intelligent apps.
Infrastructure enablers
If you plan to run and orchestrate AI workloads within Kubernetes, the Kaito solution (
https://github.com/kaito-project/kaito
) may be of great help. It is available as a managed add-on
for AKS. Kaito essentially helps deploy and run AI/ML models on Kubernetes clusters by handling dynamic scaling and GPU demand at runtime.
Azure OpenAI models are made available in two different ways:
Serverless endpoints
: This deployment model is fully managed by Microsoft and follows a pay-as-you-go
token-based billing, typically measured in
Tokens per Minute
(
TPM
). The TPM deployment model is subject to default subscription quotas, which can result in throttling once those limits are reached.
Provisioned throughput
: This model is also fully managed but comes with reserved and dedicated capacity. Cost and performance are predictable.
Other models can be deployed through the poorly named Managed Compute deployment model, which is actually not managed by Microsoft but by the customer. It requires extra effort to get a model ready for its first inference. Go for this if you want to control and manage the end-to-end AI infrastructure or if you want to use a model that is not made available through Azure OpenAI.
The next section explores important topics that are commonly used in AI solutions.
Embeddings generation, vector databases, and hybrid search
We can’t talk
about GenAI without explaining the concept of
embeddings. The rise of deep learning caused
the widespread adoption of the text-embedding concept.
Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers
(
BERT
) is, among others, a transformer-based deep learning model that is able to create embeddings, which are vector-based
numeric representations, where each number is a dimension. The number of dimensions you obtain depends on the model used to generate the embeddings. In Azure OpenAI, there are a few models, such as
text-embedding-ada-002
,
text-embedding-3-large
, and
text-embedding-3-small
.
Other media types, such as images and audio, can be vectorized too, but let’s stick to text for now.
The purpose of text embeddings is to gather as much contextual information as possible and to store it in the form of numeric vectors. Vectors can be compared with others to see whether they look similar or not. Nowadays, we typically compare vectors by calculating their
cosine similarity
, although other comparison techniques exist.
Embeddings support various use cases, including handling vague or ambiguous search queries. Unlike keyword-based search, vector-based search considers the context of the query, enhancing semantic understanding. This allows search engines—or any system processing natural language queries—to retrieve more relevant results from a vector database.
Here is a small example that should help you understand the concept. The following two sentences contain the word
crane
:
The construction supervisor leased a crane for the building project.
I learned about the migration patterns of the sandhill crane. These birds are wonderful!
As human beings, we instantly identify the context and we understand that, despite
crane
being the same word
in both sentences, it has a totally different meaning because
of the context in which it is used. That is
what transformers such as BERT help identify. BERT would capture the broader context around the word
crane
. Let’s use Azure OpenAI to vectorize and calculate the cosine similarity between our two sentences. We can run this piece of Python code (truncated for clarity):
print
(cosine_similarity(
    openai.Embedding.create(
input
=
"The construction supervisor leased a crane for the building project."
,
        engine=
"embedding"
)[
'data'
][
0
][
'embedding'
],
    openai.Embedding.create(
input
=
"I learned about the migration patterns of the sandhill crane. These birds are wonderful!"
,
        engine=
"
embedding"
)[
'data'
][
0
][
'embedding'
]))
With Azure OpenAI, the result of the preceding code is about 0.76, indicating a fairly high level of similarity between the two sentences. A score near 0 suggests low to no similarity, while a score near 1 indicates strong similarity. The exact score may vary depending on the model used to generate the embeddings and the number of vector dimensions.
In traditional probabilities, a score of 0.76 is rather high, but this principle doesn’t apply to cosine similarity. The cosine similarity score shouldn’t be treated as an absolute indicator of a true match between two pieces of text. Instead, it’s more meaningful to sort scores in descending order and focus on the highest ones to determine the most relevant matches. For example, let’s consider the following sentences stored in a Python DataFrame and their corresponding vectors:
Figure 8.4 – Sentences and their corresponding vectors
Admittedly, these
sentences are quite different and do
not convey the same meaning. We can take the top two
results of a search query:
df[
"cs"
] = df.ada_v2.apply(
lambda
x: cosine_similarity(
        x,
        generate_embeddings(
"I'm looking for information about climate change?"
)
    )
)
print
(
    df.sort_values(
"cs"
, ascending=
False
).head(
2
)
)
This would give us the closest matches, regardless of the actual value of the cosine similarity score.
Figure 8.5 – Search results using cosine similarity
A keyword-based method alone would be unlikely to return these results as the word
climate
present in our query is not present in the data itself. The preceding examples demonstrate how to compute embeddings and perform vector queries using a Python dataframe. In real-world scenarios, however, the objective is to store vectors—representing the relevant knowledge domain—in a
persistent
storage solution, such as a vector database, for future retrieval and use.
In Azure, several services can act as vector databases, enabling support for vector search queries. One of the earliest was AI Search, which introduced hybrid search—a combination of vector-based and keyword-based search capabilities. Although vector search offers greater semantic understanding, keyword search remains valuable in cases involving highly specialized terminology or scenarios such as querying
specific IT incident numbers and product identifiers.
However, Azure’s
capabilities in this area have evolved rapidly, making hybrid search no longer a key differentiator. Instead, you should choose a service based
on other factors, such as scalability, performance, and costs.
Table 8.3
considers these factors against a vector database storing about 10 million vectors. The numbers come from Microsoft’s documentation, except for the cost part:
Scalability
Performance (P95)
Costs
AI Search
Horizontal - Partitions * Replicas
< 150 ms
If the expected
Queries Per Second
(
QPS
) is high, costs will rise quickly.
Cosmos DB
Horizontal - Logical Partitions
< 20 ms
Low if logical partitions are properly designed, else high to very high.
Azure SQL
Vertical (Hyperscale 100 TB max)
< 300 ms
High
PostgreSQL + pgvector
Vertical (Hyperscale)
~ 500 ms
High
Redis Enterprise (no persistent state)
Horizontal
< 200 ms for 1 billion vectors
High
Azure Databricks
Horizontal
< 500 ms for 1 billion vectors
Moderate
Table 8.3 – Comparing vector databases
These numbers should, of course, be taken with a grain of salt—they represent a snapshot in time and are subject to change. However, one clear takeaway from the comparison is that if ultra-low latency is a priority, Redis is a strong candidate. Another important aspect is whether the vector database scales vertically
or horizontally. As with many other domains, vertical scaling tends to become a bottleneck over time, whereas horizontal scaling offers more long-term flexibility and resilience.
Ultimately, solution
architecture is always about trade-offs—you need to weigh what matters most for your specific use case. For instance, you should always examine
the QPS a service can sustain. If you’re building a customer-facing chatbot embedded in a mobile app used by millions, a high QPS capability becomes a top priority, potentially outweighing other factors. In our experience, the challenges we faced in most GenAI projects were not about AI itself but about how to make it scalable, secure, and still affordable in terms of costs.
Let’s now explore a few AI patterns and use cases.
Zooming in on AI patterns and use cases
Figure 8.6
illustrates the
most common AI patterns and use cases:
Figure 8.6 – AI patterns and use cases
Before diving
deeper into the patterns, let’s first examine the key concepts that underpin most of them, namely, embeddings, vector databases, and hybrid search.
Retrieval augmentation generation
LLMs are
trained on a huge amount of training data
and parameters. However, they know nothing about your data, unless that data was made public. To compensate for this, the RAG pattern emerged and gained popularity shortly after the rise of GenAI. In essence, it enhances LLMs by incorporating external knowledge—such as your organization’s data—into the generation process. It has been particularly used in
chat with your data
scenarios, although it is not restricted to chatbots.
Figure 8.7
shows two variants of a RAG workflow.
Figure 8.7 – RAG workflow
One way to
achieve RAG for a chatbot is to follow these steps:
The user/system
sends a query to the orchestration API.
The orchestration API retrieves the user query, converts it into a vector (embeddings), and performs a search query against a vector database to find the closest matches (typically using cosine similarity).
Next, the orchestration API forwards both the user query and the retrieved search results to the LLM. The LLM evaluates whether the retrieved information aligns with the query and then generates a coherent, human-readable response.
The alternative approach involves first verifying whether the user’s initial query falls within the chatbot’s scope before proceeding with the vector search. A possible Azure RAG architecture is illustrated in
Figure 8.8
:
Figure 8.8 – Possible RAG architecture
The frontend
talks to the chat API through
Azure Application Gateway (the WAF) and
Azure API Management
(
APIM
). The chat API queries the vector database (AI Search) and sends both the results and the
user query to the LLM through API Management, which plays the role of AI gateway. Microsoft has published numerous policies that help deal with AI scenarios, such as load balancing between LLM instances, dealing with token limits, and semantic caching. Additionally, shielding the LLM from the rest of the world is a good practice as APIM can perform some extra token validation —or apply any other policy—while authenticating to the LLM with its managed identity.
AI Search indexers can be used to crawl the prepared data available in a storage account, and we can leverage AI Search’s built-in
embedding skill
capability to generate the embeddings. This skill must be linked to an embedding model made available through a separate LLM instance. So, in
Figure 8.8
, we use an instance for the conversation aspects and another one for the embedding generation. For simple scenarios, the built-in embedding skill is good enough, and it is possible to create a custom embedding skill for more advanced scenarios. One of the benefits of AI Search as a vector database compared to other vector databases is the possibility of leveraging the indexers to fill the vector indexes instead of
having to push vector data ourselves. You may add an extra component to the mix to store the conversation history and the possibility of resuming older conversations.
From a code
perspective, we can use Semantic Kernel, a Microsoft SDK, to deal with the conversation orchestration and to abstract away the AI Search vector database using Semantic Kernel vector stores. This abstraction layer makes it easy to switch to another vector database should the application evolve.
Finally,
Server-Sent Events
(
SSEs
) can be
used to stream tokens from the LLMs and progressively display the response on the frontend, which is more user-friendly than waiting for the LLM to generate the full answer. As of June 2025, the GPT-4o Realtime API is in public preview and currently supports only speech and audio, not text. Therefore, SSE remains the most effective approach for streaming text responses. Naturally, this choice should be re-evaluated when considering how best to interact with LLMs in the future.
It’s worth noting that the
RAG pattern has several variants, including
GraphRAG
and
LightRAG
, which aim to address
some of the traditional limitations of standard RAG implementations—such as limited contextual understanding and inefficiencies in retrieval and reasoning. Needless to say, regardless of the chosen pattern, the quality of the source data plays a key role in the accuracy of the RAG solution.
Let’s now explore another AI pattern.
Cache augmentation generation
One of the limitations of RAG is its reliance on a vector database with vector or hybrid search capabilities. This retrieval
step can introduce a
performance overhead that may not always be justified. While such a trade-off can be acceptable when working with hundreds of terabytes of data, it becomes less desirable when dealing with smaller knowledge sets, where the cost of retrieval may outweigh the benefits.
The
Cache Augmentation Generation
(
CAG
) pattern is a good alternative as it eliminates the need for a vector database or any other vector memory. It leverages the LLM’s large context window to send the entire knowledge base to the LLM. A context window is the maximum number of tokens LLMs can swallow in one go.
More specifically, we can override the LMM’s KV cache with our knowledge data, once and for all. Any further interaction with the LLM will not require including extra context, and the LLM alone is enough as it has the entire knowledge domain in its cache.
Figure 8.9
provides a simplified view of CAG.
Figure 8.9 – CAG workflow
The first step consists of preparing the knowledge data and uploading it into the LLM’s KV cache. The second and
third steps simply interact with the LLM to query the knowledge domain. Some open source frameworks make this possible, but as of June 2025, there is no
true CAG support in Azure. It is nevertheless interesting to be aware of the pattern as it can be a useful alternative to RAG in some scenarios and could become available in the future.
CAG is particularly
useful for smaller knowledge domains that can fit the context window of an LLM. Beyond RAG and CAG, AI solutions often require extra tooling, which brings us to
Tool-Augmented Generation
(
TAG
), our next pattern.
Tool-augmented generation
TAG has existed since the early days of GenAI. Its core purpose is to enhance an AI solution with additional tools
that enable it to perform actions. A practical
example of this is GPT Actions in ChatGPT. They allow you to define your own agent and
connect it to custom APIs by importing the OpenAPI definition into the action. Once configured, your custom GPT implementation can autonomously interact with those APIs based on the prompts and logic you defined.
Similarly, Azure OpenAI supports
function calling
—a technique that involves injecting a function definition into the prompt and letting the LLM decide when it is appropriate to invoke it. For example, when using Assistant Playground in AI Foundry, you can easily add a function like this one:
{
"name"
:
"get_weather"
,
"description"
:
"Determine weather in my location"
,
"parameters"
:
{
"type"
:
"object"
,
"properties"
:
{
"location"
:
{
"
type"
:
"string"
,
"description"
:
"The city and state e.g. Seattle, WA"
},
"unit"
:
{
"type"
:
"string"
,
"enum"
:
[
"c"
,
"f"
]
}
},
"
required"
:
[
"location"
]
},
"strict"
:
false
}
In your first
interaction with the LLM, you must inject
this function signature through an appropriate prompt. The function signature makes it clear that the
location
parameter is required for every weather forecast request. In such a case, you could ask the following question to the LLM:
What's the weather today?
It would most probably reply something like this:
Could you tell me your location…
Complete your answer:
Brussels
Then, it will be capable of crafting the function call that needs to be made in order to obtain the answer:
get_weather({"location":"Brussels"})
It’s worth mentioning that the LLM never directly executes a function itself. Instead, it returns a structured response indicating which function should be called and with what parameters. In the preceding example, you might have noticed that the LLM performed NER (which we discussed earlier) and captured the
Brussels
entity to pass it to the function. It is then up to your application code to evaluate this response, perform the actual function call, and feed the result back to the LLM to complete the interaction.
TAG is nothing
more than that. Today, there are many tools that specialize in specific tasks such as converting natural language into SQL queries, and so on. Frameworks
such as Semantic Kernel help abstract the injection of the function signature. For example, a function declaration in Semantic Kernel may look like this:
var
plugin = kernel.ImportPluginFromObject(
new
MyTools(),
"MyTools"
);
public
class
MyTools
{
    [
KernelFunction
]
public
async
Task<
string
>
GetWeather
(
string
location
)
=> ...
}
It instructs Semantic Kernel to inject the corresponding function signature into the prompt sent to the LLM, making the model aware of the function’s availability. This code is more maintainable than directly injecting the function signature but ultimately ends up with the same result.
You can equip your AI solution with as many tools as needed, but this quickly introduces challenges that go beyond AI itself—particularly around scalability and security.
For example, an AI application may have to interact with a variety of services, as shown in
Figure 8.10
.
Figure 8.10 – TAG application calling many different tools
Each target system might require different authentication mechanisms and might be based on different protocols. This can be challenging at scale.
Additionally, from a security point of view, how do you ensure that a user interacting with a chat interface has the
appropriate permissions to perform actions A or B? This concern also applies to RAG and its variants. If your RAG solution ingests
an entire SharePoint repository—where access to documents is typically security-trimmed—how can you guarantee that the user asking a question is actually authorized to receive the answer? How do you ensure that your RAG implementation respects those same access controls? The more you build and scale AI solutions, the more often you’ll encounter these types of real-world constraints.
In conclusion, while TAG
brings greater capabilities to any AI solution, it lacks a standard way to interact with remote systems. A partial answer to this is
Model Context Protocol
(
MCP
), which we will discuss in the next section.
Model context protocol
As of June 2025, MCP remains
an emerging pattern
and is highly subject to change in the future, but the philosophy behind it should remain the same.
As noted in the previous section, implementing the TAG pattern at scale can be challenging, since an AI application may need to interface with numerous services, each with its own quirks. This complexity becomes difficult to manage.
MCP partially addresses this challenge by defining a standardized way to describe features and tools. Each tool can implement its own MCP server, while each consuming service maintains its own set of MCP clients. These clients and servers communicate using a common protocol, ensuring consistent interaction patterns.
At this stage, the relationship between each client and server is one to one. The MCP server acts as an abstraction layer over the underlying remote service—such as a database or an API—and encapsulates the logic required to interact with that service.
The application remains the orchestrator between the LLM and its set of MCP resources. However, it no longer needs to implement custom, ad hoc interactions for each individual service and can instead rely on MCP to standardize those interactions.
Figure 8.11
provides a high-level view of MCP:
Figure 8.11 – High-level view of an MCP-enabled application
In
Figure 8.11
, the application uses its various MCP clients to retrieve metadata about the tools and resources exposed by their corresponding MCP servers. At this stage of MCP adoption, most AI solutions host both MCP clients and servers themselves. This approach is primarily driven by security concerns, as full trust in MCP—particularly from a security standpoint—has not yet been established.
Typically, service owners develop the MCP server and distribute it as a container image or a package that can be
integrated with different AI solutions.
An MCP server
will typically expose at least the following operations:
@server.list_tools()
@server.list_prompts()
@server.call_tool()
@server.get_prompt()
These operations allow clients to fetch information and call the tools exposed by the server.
While running a local MCP server is appropriate for accessing local resources, it becomes less suitable for interacting with remote resources exposed by remote services.
The concept of a remote MCP server exists in the MCP specification but is not yet mainstream due to the lack of security. However, in the Azure ecosystem, APIM positions itself as a possible solution for remote MCP servers, as shown in
Figure 8.12
.
Figure 8.12 – Using APIM as a remote MCP server
APIM can automatically
generate the MCP server layer for any API already
published to a given APIM instance. In addition, APIM policies can be used to enforce security measures such as JWT token validation and throttling—ensuring that the underlying remote systems are securely exposed.
APIM can also act as a proxy for existing remote MCP servers, helping to standardize how such servers are exposed and consumed across different environments. When integrated with Application Insights, it provides centralized logging and monitoring of all MCP client/server interactions.
While the MCP ecosystem is still evolving, it’s expected that remote MCP servers will become the standard, with local MCP servers being limited to accessing strictly local resources.
Next, let’s explore agentic AI.
Agentic AI
Agentic AI goes beyond being a simple design pattern—it encompasses any autonomous AI system capable of making
decisions and performing tasks without human intervention. For instance, an agent could independently book a hotel room by evaluating factors such as price, comfort, and availability. A key characteristic of agentic AI is the ability of agents to tackle complex tasks, which requires them to reason and formulate a plan to achieve the desired outcome.
An example of a
complex task could be the autonomous planning of a technical intervention of a malfunctioning fridge in a retail store (see the smart fridge monitoring in retail stores use case in
Chapter 7
), detected by a monitoring system. The agent would be triggered by the alert. It should be able to find the most appropriate technician, check availability, and so on. It would take multiple steps and important coordination to make this possible.
However, to achieve this, the agent would most probably leverage a few of the patterns we have learned about so far, such as RAG (knowledge), persist state, TAG (tooling), and MCP (context and tooling), to augment the agent’s capabilities. An agent might rely on other agents to make a decision. This is called multi-agent orchestration. The context, tooling, reasoning, and knowledge can be added using low-code or pro-code solutions.
If you’re just getting started with agentic AI, the Copilot Studio portal is an excellent entry point. It offers a low-code approach that makes it easier to grasp the principles behind agentic AI and achieve meaningful results without the complexity of building everything from scratch.
Copilot Studio integrates seamlessly with the broader Microsoft ecosystem, enabling you to build event-driven agents that can respond to specific triggers—such as receiving an email or the creation of a SharePoint document. You can also schedule agent activities using the
recurrence
trigger type, allowing agents to run at predefined intervals.
For code-based solutions, Semantic Kernel is Microsoft’s open source, enterprise-ready framework designed to orchestrate and manage both single- and multi-agent workflows. In contrast, AutoGen, developed by Microsoft Research, explores more advanced and experimental scenarios but is still evolving and subject to change. For building robust enterprise-grade solutions, Semantic Kernel is the recommended choice.
Semantic Kernel makes it easy to establish communication between agents thanks to different orchestration mechanisms, as illustrated in
Figure 8.13
.
Figure 8.13 – Orchestration types in Semantic Kernel
The
following is an
explanation of these orchestration types:
Sequential orchestration
: Agents pass tasks along a pipeline. This is typically required for multi-stage reasoning.
Group chat
: Agents are part of a group and are able to communicate with each other until the termination state, configured by a termination strategy, is reached.
Concurrent orchestration
: Multiple agents work on the same task in parallel.
Handoff orchestration
: Agents
can
transfer control to other agents in the pipeline if needed. All agents know each other.
Magentic orchestration
: This orchestration model is inherited from AutoGen’s Magentic-One and is aimed at solving complex business tasks by appointing a central lead agent that coordinates workflow activities. Other agents do not know each other.
Semantic Kernel is available for .NET, Java, and Python. It’s worth exploring Semantic Kernel’s GitHub repo:
https://github.com/microsoft/semantic-kernel
.
The world of agentic AI involves many moving parts, but Dapr—which we explored in
Chapter 5
,
Working with Other Container Services
—also offers native support for agents. Dapr Agents leverages core Dapr building blocks such as service-to-service invocation, pub/sub messaging, state management, durable workflows, and the actor model to create both stateless and stateful agents. If you’re already using Dapr to develop distributed applications, its built-in support for agentic AI is certainly worth exploring. Supported patterns and orchestration types are documented in this GitHub repo:
https://github.com/dapr/dapr-agents
.
In essence, that is
all we could safely say about the core principles of
agentic AI at this stage. We prefer not to dive too much into technical details as frameworks and tools keep changing on a weekly basis.
Next, let’s explore the AI gateway feature of APIM, which can play a key role in whatever AI solution you’re trying to build.
AI gateway through APIM
As discussed earlier, in the
Model context protocol
section, APIM has already embraced the MCP model by positioning
itself as a remote MCP server for APIs published on APIM, or as a proxy to other MCP servers. However, its involvement in the GenAI space goes even further.
The extra
capabilities are part of the API gateway referred to as the AI gateway, but this is the same gateway as for non-AI solutions.
Figure 8.14
shows a subset of capabilities made available by APIM’s AI gateway:
Figure 8.14 – A few AI gateway capabilities
Here is a detailed explanation of each pattern:
Load balancing
: This pattern leverages APIM’s backend pool feature by registering multiple LLMs as backends
of the pool. Additionally, a custom retry policy can be used in case of transient failures of one of the backends or in case of throttling from the LLM. The load-balancing algorithm defaults to round-robin but can be influenced by setting priority and weight-based routing.
Model routing
: At first glance, this pattern might resemble the load-balancing one, but it fulfills a completely
different need. In essence, this pattern consists of routing the request to the appropriate model (different model versions, such as GPT-4o, GPT-3.5, etc.) based on request parameters.
Semantic caching
: This pattern
enables caching of LLM responses in an external store such as Redis, allowing subsequent requests to check the cache for similar prompts before querying the LLM again.
Rate limiting
: This pattern
allows enforcing rate-limiting policies prior to forwarding traffic to the LLMs. This is a good protection against LLMs’ abusive usage.
Message storing
: This pattern
leverages the log-to-eventhub policy to send all messages to Event Hubs. From there on, you can stream those messages with Stream Analytics or other services to get these messages landing in an operational or analytical data store.
Response streaming
: This pattern
ensures that SSE headers are added to the response when needed.
It’s worth
noting that these patterns can be combined, as most are not mutually exclusive.
While additional
patterns exist, this provides a solid foundation to begin with. The key takeaway about the AI gateway is that it shields the client from temporary LLM outages, throttling, and even changes in the underlying model. APIM serves as a bridge between the client and the target LLMs, and it can also handle authentication on their behalf—minimizing direct exposure of the LLMs themselves.
Now, let’s explore a use case.
Use case – AI-driven rule management for tax data processing
Here is
the scenario and first analysis:
Contoso is a well-established company operating in the tax domain. Their core business involves receiving tax transaction files from customers that must undergo validation, cleansing, and compliance checks before being submitted to local tax authorities. The data transformation and compliance rules are highly complex and differ significantly across countries.
Given the high volume of data and the need for low-latency processing, Contoso’s data platform is built on Azure Databricks, which provides the scalability and performance required for large-scale data transformation workloads. The heavy-lifting data transformation process is done by Databricks, but the business performs the final review prior to submitting the resulting files to the authorities.
However, because regulations keep changing, Contoso would like to gain greater agility and aims to empower business users to define these transformation and validation rules using natural language. They currently have a rule engine, but it lacks flexibility and is not intuitive enough. By leveraging AI, the organization seeks to translate these natural language inputs into executable rules that can be integrated back into their underlying data platform.
Before these AI-generated rules are injected into production pipelines, Contoso would like to simulate their application against representative data samples to verify correctness and effectiveness.
Let’s first
extract the keywords in this scenario that demand our focus:
Define data transformation rules using natural language
: Business users would like to define rules by simply expressing them in natural language. This means that we must foresee an NLP-friendly AI model to help us.
Inline validation and injection of the generated rules back into the Azure Databricks data platform
: Databricks performs the heavy-lifting data transformation using broader rulesets that apply to specific countries. We must be able to enrich those rulesets through a feedback loop mechanism.
After some investigation and prompt engineering techniques, we conclude that LLMs are not that good at checking and adjusting data by themselves, especially when dealing with numbers of all types (double, integer, and so on) and calculations. Instead, we realize that we could leverage LLMs to generate PySpark code snippets that implement the rules defined by end users, which we could reinject into Databricks. We go to the drawing board and end up with the high-level view shown in
Figure 8.15
, discussed in the next section.
Diagrams
Figure 8.15
illustrates a
possible architecture for this use case.
Figure 8.15 – Possible architecture for the use case
Users interact
with a user interface to monitor ongoing operations and perform pre- and post-Databricks checks, along with optional additional data transformations. The interface may include a chat window, allowing users to request the application
of new transformation rules. These requests are sent from the browser to a
Backend for Frontend
(
BFF
), if used, which then forwards them to the chat API. The chat API communicates with the LLM via the AI gateway to generate the corresponding PySpark code.
Effective prompt engineering is essential to ensure that the LLM returns only the relevant code snippet. Once received, the generated code can be displayed to the user, who may choose to execute an in-cluster test against a sample dataset and review the output.
If the user approves the result, the code can be committed to a Git repository—triggering an Azure DevOps pipeline (or an equivalent CI/CD tool) to run a Databricks job on a larger dataset.
This use case demonstrates the value of leveraging code generation rather than relying on the LLM to analyze the data directly. A more effective approach is to express the desired rules and have the LLM generate the corresponding code to implement them.
Let’s now summarize what we have learned in the chapter.
Summary
This chapter has shown that while GenAI is transforming how we build intelligent applications, traditional AI remains highly relevant for structured, cost-effective, and high-precision tasks. We explored how Azure supports both paradigms through services such as Azure ML, AI Vision, and AI Language, and how foundational NLP techniques such as tokenization, POS tagging, and entity recognition still underpin many modern solutions.
We examined the growing role of embeddings and vector search in GenAI scenarios, comparing Azure’s options and trade-offs for storing and querying vector data. Key architectural patterns, such as RAG, CAG, and TAG, were discussed, each addressing different constraints around data access, performance, and actionability. MCP was introduced as an emerging solution to standardize interactions between AI agents and external tools, helping reduce complexity at scale.
Agentic AI was explored as the natural evolution of these patterns, combining reasoning, planning, and orchestration to tackle complex tasks autonomously. Frameworks such as Semantic Kernel, Dapr, and tools such as Copilot Studio illustrate how both pro-code and low-code solutions can support this shift. Finally, the AI gateway capabilities of APIM were highlighted as essential for building scalable, secure, and resilient AI architectures.
The chapter closed with a practical use case that illustrated how LLMs can generate executable PySpark code from natural language inputs, enabling a feedback loop between business users and data pipelines. Rather than relying on LLMs to interpret data directly, empowering them to generate code offers a more reliable, testable, and maintainable path forward.
In the next chapter, we will turn our focus to security architecture.
Chapter 9
will guide you through the principles, patterns, and native Azure capabilities you can use to build secure-by-design architectures that remain resilient against evolving threats.

9
Security Architecture
Security is a foundational pillar of any cloud architecture, and in Azure, it encompasses a broad set of capabilities and shared responsibilities across identity, data, infrastructure, and operational practices. In this chapter, we explore the key aspects of securing solutions on Azure, focusing on
Identity and Access Management
(
IDAM
) through Entra ID and modern authentication protocols; data security through encryption, classification, and governance; and
Cloud Security Posture Management
(
CSPM
), as well as
Cloud Workload Protection Platforms
(
CWPPs
), to ensure continuous compliance and threat visibility.
We will also cover DevSecOps practices, including
Static Application Security Testing
(
SAST
) and
Dynamic Application Security Testing
(
DAST
), and the integration of security controls in CI/CD pipelines.
Operational Security
(
SecOps
) is addressed through the use of
Security Information Event Management
(
SIEM
) and
Security Orchestration Automation and Response
(
SOAR
) tools such as Microsoft Sentinel and Defender for Cloud.
More specifically, we’ll look at the following topics:
Introducing cloud-native security and cloud-native security principles
Laying the foundations for cloud security
Zooming in on data security
Zooming in on identity
Zooming in on CSPM
Zooming in on CWPPs
Zooming in on DevSecOps
Zooming in on SecOps
Zooming in on appliances and CASB solutions
While the network remains a critical security layer, it has already been covered in detail in
Chapter 3
,
Infrastructure Design
, and
Chapter 4
,
Working with Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS)
. Therefore, in this chapter, we’ll assume that you are routing and filtering traffic appropriately and using Private Link all the way.
The
availability
aspect of the
Confidentiality, Integrity, and Availability
(
CIA
) triad was thoroughly addressed in
Chapter 3
, where we discussed the various options, such as availability zones, geo-replication, and backup/restore capabilities. This chapter will instead focus primarily on
confidentiality
and
integrity
, which are just as essential in building secure cloud-native applications.
Let us now explore the technical requirements.
Technical requirements
There are no technical requirements for this chapter. The maps and diagrams are available at
https://github.com/PacktPublishing/The-Azure-Cloud-Native-Architecture-Mapbook-Second-Edition/tree/main/Chapter09
.
Introducing cloud-native security
As we’ve seen in the previous
chapters, the cloud offers significant advantages in accelerating solution development and deployment while optimizing costs. However, these benefits can only be fully realized if we also modernize how we approach security. Cloud-native security embraces the shift-left principle, which emphasizes incorporating security earlier in the asset life cycle. Addressing security from the outset helps avoid late-stage issues and deployment delays. That said, implementing this approach is often more challenging than it sounds.
In many cases, developers—particularly early adopters—and infrastructure teams have adopted modern practices such as infrastructure as code, while security teams remain entrenched in traditional, reactive, and
waterfall-driven processes. It’s not uncommon for firewall rule changes to take days to be approved and implemented, which stands in stark contrast to the shift-left philosophy and undermines the progress made by other teams.
With the right tools and technologies, security has become declarative. As discussed in
Chapter 3
,
Infrastructure Design
, and
Chapter 4
,
Working with Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS)
, CI/CD pipelines enable organizations to provision both code and infrastructure consistently. The same automation can be extended to security, especially in cloud-native platforms such as Kubernetes, where elements such as layer 4 (transport) and layer 7 (application) policies are defined as code and automated.
However, cloud-native security is not just about tools and technology—it requires a cultural shift. Organizations must rethink their structure and embrace a new mindset, which can be difficult in environments with deep-rooted legacy practices. Regardless of your industry or regulatory landscape, there are always opportunities to improve automation and streamline security processes. Regulations typically define
what
must be achieved, not
how
to achieve it. Many organizations in the insurance and financial industries entirely rely on manual processes and lengthy approval steps before opening a flow between a source and a target. While this is understandable, it dramatically impacts the time to market and agility.
In a cloud-native model, security architects must create value by enabling automation and ensuring that security processes do not hinder progress. They should serve as
enablers
—educating other teams on security’s importance, offering practical solutions, and supporting risk-based decisions. They must assess risks objectively and allow the business to determine which residual risks are acceptable. They should contribute to architectural patterns that encompass the security dimension so as to quicken approval processes.
This mindset shift is particularly difficult in risk-averse sectors such as banking, where cautiousness is ingrained. The degree to which an organization can evolve from traditional to cloud-native security practices often depends on its inherent culture. But one thing is clear: while cloud technologies can deliver on promises of cost efficiency, speed, resilience, and scalability, these outcomes are
unreachable
without a fundamental change in security practices. Security is a core pillar of cloud success—or failure.
From a technical standpoint, there has also been a significant shift toward identity as the primary layer of defense rather than the network, although Microsoft has closed many gaps in terms of network capabilities over
recent years. However, network-based security still tends to be more complex, costly, and rigid. Unfortunately, many traditional security architects continue to rely heavily on perimeter-focused strategies (the DMZ mindset), often overlooking identity-based security.
In this chapter, we will offer guidance on adopting a cloud-native security posture and highlight traditional approaches that may hinder your cloud journey. Keep in mind that your ability to succeed in the cloud is closely tied to your ability to modernize your security practices.
Let’s explore some key guidelines to help establish the right mindset and lay strong foundations for your cloud security journey.
Laying the foundations for cloud security
After years of working extensively with Azure, I’ve observed a recurring pattern across many organizations: security teams
often replicate their on-premises security stack in the cloud without adaptation. Familiar names such as F5, Netskope, Palo Alto firewalls, Splunk, QRadar, and CyberArk frequently make their way into cloud environments
by default
. While these established tools are not inherently problematic, they often introduce additional complexity and are adopted without evaluating Microsoft-native alternatives.
The rationale is usually the same: the desire for a unified
single pane of glass
across environments, or statements such as: “
Palo Alto firewalls are better than Azure Firewall
.” While it is true that niche products are often richer than Azure alternatives, too often, decisions are driven by product familiarity and internal expertise rather than actual business, regulatory, or technical requirements. A key contributor to this trend is the lack of Azure/Microsoft-specific expertise within security teams. How many certified Azure security professionals can you typically find in a CISO organization? Think about your own organization and you will be surprised by the number. It’s often close to zero.
The single-pane-of-glass argument is very valid and used to justify relying on legacy or third-party security tools across environments. However, it’s important to recognize that many Azure-native services are now
designed to work beyond Azure, supporting hybrid and multi-cloud scenarios as well. Microsoft has gradually renamed several services to emphasize that they are no longer limited to Azure. As for concerns about feature gaps between Azure-native products and third-party solutions, Microsoft has consistently shown a strong pace of innovation, rapidly closing gaps, and introducing new capabilities. Once a native service gains traction, feature parity with more established competitors is often reached sooner than expected.
The added complexity of using non-native products stems from the operational overhead they introduce. When relying on third-party tools, organizations are fully responsible for ensuring high availability, scalability, and disaster recovery—capabilities that are typically built into Azure services natively and managed by Microsoft. Additionally, native solutions offer tighter and better integration, simplified management, and higher resilience, allowing teams to focus more on outcomes rather than infrastructure.
Here are a few principles that might help trigger the correct behavior when designing security solutions in Azure and Kubernetes:
Know the risk appetite of your organization
: Security decisions should primarily be guided by the organization’s risk appetite and business drivers. There is no one-size-fits-all approach—what works for a bank with limited risk tolerance will differ significantly from a digital-native company offering entertainment services.
Best of suite over best of breed
: Whatever security challenge you’re addressing, always start by evaluating the native Azure offering and how well it integrates with the broader Azure ecosystem. For example, most Azure services are designed to work seamlessly with Azure Key Vault for managing secrets, certificates, and encryption keys. Choosing a third-party vault solution could introduce friction or limit this native integration. Furthermore, all Azure services support full automation through infrastructure as code. If you opt for custom or third-party solutions, you must ensure they align with these automation capabilities and evaluate how they integrate
with the Azure ecosystem, to avoid disrupting your cloud experience. Following this principle is even more important in container orchestration platforms.
Assess trust in Microsoft and clearly identify your responsibilities
: The cloud operates on a shared responsibility model, which inherently means relinquishing full control over certain aspects of the infrastructure. While this may seem straightforward, many security teams struggle to fully embrace this reality. Rather than attempting to uncover the inner workings of multi-tenant service offerings, organizations should focus on clearly understanding and addressing their own areas of responsibility within the model. This shift in mindset is essential for building an effective and sustainable cloud security posture. The other aspects that you do not control should be covered by SLAs and Microsoft’s liability with regard to security breaches that may affect your organization. It is then up to your legal department to evaluate this part.
Conduct thorough risk assessments and document residual risks
: Many security teams still lack robust risk assessment practices. A common example is the handling of container image vulnerabilities. It’s not unusual to see blanket policies—such as
zero high vulnerabilities
—that block
deployments without evaluating the actual risk or context. This rigid approach often leads to unnecessary friction and delays. A more effective strategy involves assessing whether identified vulnerabilities are exploitable within your specific environment, considering other mitigating controls already in place. In some cases, it may be entirely acceptable to deploy containers with high-severity vulnerabilities if they pose no real threat under current conditions. This observation applies to everything you do in Azure, not just containers.
Adapting CISO practices to cloud-native realities
: Shift left and DevSecOps are far more than industry buzzwords—they are critical enablers for adopting cloud-native practices and unlocking the full potential of the cloud. However, their effectiveness is limited if CISO-led security processes are not reimagined to align with the realities of modern, inherently disruptive IT environments. In a world where everything is defined as code, developers and infrastructure engineers can now implement network and service mesh policies directly. While these actions must remain under the
CISO’s oversight, they are incompatible with traditional ticket-based workflows. This highlights the urgent need for the CISO function to evolve and embrace new operational models that support agility without compromising compliance.
Choose appropriate compliance frameworks
: When selecting a security framework, choose one that aligns well with cloud environments and with local regulatory obligations. For instance, ISO/IEC 27001:2013 does not fully account for the shared responsibility model, as it includes controls related to physical infrastructure—something cloud customers typically cannot influence. If you choose ISO, consider adopting the 2022 revision, which better reflects modern IT realities. Alternatively, frameworks such as CIS Controls v8.1 offer more actionable guidance for assessing cloud-specific hardening and SecOps. Remember, frameworks are not mutually exclusive—you can combine them to cover different aspects of your security posture. Make sure to stay away from any in-house compliance frameworks as they will likely not be relevant at all for a cloud environment.
This list of fundamental principles is not exhaustive, and the degree to which they should be applied will depend on the specific Azure and Kubernetes footprint you intend to establish in the cloud, and on your risk appetite. Let’s now explore the Security Architecture Map.

Map, shown in
Figure 9.1
, should help you deal with the typical duties of an Azure security architect:

: In this section, we’ll explore the different encryption mechanisms, as well as key stores
such as Azure Key Vault and
Hardware Security Module

) options. We’ll also talk about Microsoft Purview, which has become the
data governance Swiss Army knife.

), as well as how to combine different mechanisms to achieve

: This section provides an overview of Azure’s CSPM capabilities, focusing on how tools such as Microsoft Defender for Cloud (foundational) help continuously assess, monitor, and improve the security posture of your Azure environment by identifying misconfigurations, enforcing best practices, and ensuring compliance with regulatory standards.

: This section highlights how Defender can be used to protect running workloads.

: This section introduces Azure’s SIEM and SOAR capabilities through Microsoft Sentinel, highlighting how it enables centralized threat detection, investigation, and response. It covers how automation and orchestration help reduce response times, improve incident handling, and enhance overall security operations efficiency.
DevSecOps
: This section explores the integration of security into the development life cycle through DevSecOps practices. It highlights which technologies and services are used to embed security checks, policy enforcement, and compliance validation into CI/CD pipelines.
Appliances and CASB
: This section explores
a few common concerns, such as TLS inspection, firewalls, and
Web Application Firewalls
(
WAFs
), as well as introducing the
Cloud Access Security Broker
(
CASB
) concept.
As stated in the introduction, the
CIA triad is a foundational model in information security. Since we previously explored the availability dimension in detail in
Chapter 3
,
Infrastructure Design
, we will shift our focus to the remaining pillars: integrity and confidentiality.
Let’s see how to ensure confidentiality and integrity in the next section.
Zooming in on data security
In this section, we will examine
the various types of encryption and the options available for storing encryption keys. We will also cover data governance and data-specific features that help protect against integrity loss.
Figure 9.2
illustrates the different data security features:
Figure 9.2 – Data security features
Understanding encryption mechanisms and capabilities
Before diving into the specific
details, let’s first review the different encryption types and Azure-related capabilities:
Encryption at rest
: This type of
encryption safeguards data at rest (stored) by ensuring it remains unreadable if the
storage device is stolen or accessed with malicious intent. Most Azure services let you
Bring Your Own Key
(
BYOK
) or, by default, use Microsoft-managed keys to ensure data at rest is always
encrypted. An additional
possibility is
Host Your Own Key
(
HYOK
), which
means that you generate the keys and store them in hardware that you control.
Be aware that this option is mostly compatible with Infrastructure as a Service
.
Encryption in transit
: This type of encryption protects data in motion. For example, when accessing
https://portal.azure.com
using a web browser, the client initiates a TLS connection
with the Azure server. During this process, the client verifies the legitimacy of the server by validating its certificate, and both sides perform a key exchange to derive a shared symmetric encryption key. This key is then used to encrypt all subsequent data transmitted between the client and server.
Asymmetric
cryptography is used during the handshake to authenticate the server and secure the key exchange process. Simply put, this type of encryption ensures that someone sniffing the network would only see encrypted data and not clear text. As for encryption at rest, Azure services default to Microsoft-managed certificates, but most services let you
Bring Your Own Certificate
(
BYOC
). When
using BYOC, you typically store them in Azure Key Vault and integrate Azure services with Key Vault to let them pull the
certificate. You may
Host Your Own Certificate
(
HYOC
) by storing private keys in hardware that you control.
Be aware that this is absolutely not compatible with Azure PaaS services
.
Encryption in use
: This type of
encryption protects in-memory data. As with the previous example, without encryption in use, any administrator or system process of a machine might perform memory inspection and see data in clear text. In Azure, encryption in
use is offered through the broader
confidential computing
concept.
Encryption at rest and in transit in Azure rely by default on Microsoft-managed keys. This is by far the easiest way to leverage Azure services. Many companies rely on these defaults and only bring their own certificates for internet-facing web apps and APIs. From that perspective, it’s worth noting that Azure Key Vault has built-in integration mechanisms with well-known
Certificate Authorities
(
CAs
), such as GlobalSign
and DigiCert, including automatic certificate renewal.
However, many other companies have regulatory obligations to store encryption keys in HSMs or do not feel comfortable with the US Patriot Act, allowing the
National Security Agency
(
NSA
) to request data from any US company, wherever the data is stored, whether on national territory or anywhere else. Of course, companies such as Microsoft and Amazon resist these legal obligations, but the risk is
definitely not null. It is worth mentioning that this statement does not apply to Azure China, since Microsoft doesn’t operate those data centers.
For this very reason, many organizations turn to customer-managed keys to strengthen their data protection. This naturally raises the question: where should these keys be stored? How can we be safe against the previously mentioned threats?
The safest approach to mitigate all the risks linked to potential third-party interference is HYOK, where encryption keys never leave your on-premises infrastructure. Unfortunately, this approach is compatible with a very limited number of services, such as Microsoft 365. Additionally, keeping keys on-premises introduces latency and can undermine resilience by creating a tight dependency between the cloud and your on-premises infrastructure—an approach that is far from ideal.
HYOK aside, Azure comes with many different options to store your keys. The full list, along with the drivers that should help you choose the best option for your use case, is available here:
https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/security/fundamentals/key-management-choose
.
There are a few factors to consider before choosing an option:
Service model
: What service model are you primarily using—IaaS, PaaS, CaaS, or FaaS? This question alone can rule out several options. For instance, Azure Dedicated HSM doesn’t natively integrate with any Azure PaaS services, making it incompatible with that model. On the other hand, a few PaaS services support Azure Managed HSM, and nearly all of them are compatible with Azure Key Vault.
Regulatory requirements
: Regulatory requirements are often tied to
Federal Information Processing Standards
(
FIPS
) levels. Select the option that aligns most closely with your compliance obligations.
Root of trust control and key sovereignty
: Choose any of the compatible options if you require full
control over your encryption keys and want to ensure that Microsoft has no access to them.
Table 9.1
describes a few scenarios to help you choose:
Scenario
Best option
You are not a regulated industry
Azure Key Vault Standard or Premium
You are a regulated industry and you plan to use mostly PaaS services
Azure Key Vault Premium
You are a regulated industry and key sovereignty is critical, and you plan to use mostly PaaS services
Managed HSM
You plan to use IaaS (self-hosted databases, etc.) as the main service model
Any HSM option, including HYOK (with the drawbacks highlighted earlier)
You want full control over the HSM
Dedicated HSM or HYOK
Table 9.1 – Choosing a key store
Be aware that with Azure Dedicated HSM, you are fully responsible for all aspects of management, including firmware upgrades, patching, and ensuring high availability and disaster recovery. For instance, achieving zone redundancy requires you to provision two separate Dedicated HSM instances and deploy them in different availability zones. Additionally, you must also foresee all integrations yourself using the Luna client or other compatible libraries.
Now, let’s dive a bit deeper into the underlying encryption mechanisms.
Understanding data encryption keys and key encryption keys
Something important to grasp is the role
of the
Data Encryption Key
(
DEK
) and
Key Encryption Key
(
KEK
). When you BYOK, you actually bring your own KEK. This KEK never leaves the boundaries
of the HSM. Asymmetric encryption is used to encrypt the DEK through key wrap and unwrap operations. The
encrypted DEK is stored on the server side in
the Azure service, but the unencrypted symmetric key lives in memory. This means that the actual encryption key used to encrypt the data is in fact available in the memory of the servers used by the service. One way to protect in-memory information is to rely on encryption in use (discussed in the next section), which translates to confidential computing in Azure.
